I guess what brought me in is that I’ve been having these nights where, after work especially—or sometimes after a tense dinner—I start eating and it’s like… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, but the decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off.

I can be aware of what I’m doing, like I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and part of me is thinking, “You should stop now,” but I can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment. It feels really strange and upsetting afterward, because it’s not like I’m not aware—it’s more like I’m watching myself keep going and I don’t feel fully in control.

Yeah, I think so. Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.

It’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.

So, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.

Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.\n\nIt’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating during these episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Control\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that some nights after work or a tense dinner, I start eating and it feels like the decision-making part of me shuts off, like I can see myself reaching for more food but I can’t make myself stop.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.

Like some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.

I’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”

I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.

I don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.

So… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. Or if it does happen, I’m not recognizing it in the same way.

It feels really strange and upsetting afterward, because it’s not like I’m not aware—it’s more like I’m watching myself keep going and I don’t feel fully in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure I’d call it guilt every time. I mean, afterward I definitely feel bad, like really unsettled and disappointed in myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, even if no one saw it, or this heavy feeling of, “Why did I let that happen again?”\n\nIt’s not like I sit there thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed at myself. I’ll replay it a bit and feel kind of ashamed or frustrated, but the word “guilty” feels… maybe close, but I’m not sure it’s the exact word I’d pick every time.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.\n\nIt’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.

I mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.

So I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.

For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.

Sometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.

It’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.

I’ll replay it a bit and feel kind of ashamed or frustrated, but the word “guilty” feels… maybe close, but I’m not sure it’s the exact word I’d pick every time.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.

It’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.

If I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is that I’ve been having these nights where, after work especially—or sometimes after a tense dinner—I start eating and it’s like… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, but the decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off.\n\nI can be aware of what I’m doing, like I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and part of me is thinking, “You should stop now,” but I can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment. It feels really strange and upsetting afterward, because it’s not like I’m not aware—it’s more like I’m watching myself keep going and I don’t feel fully in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure I’d call it guilt every time. I mean, afterward I definitely feel bad, like really unsettled and disappointed in myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, even if no one saw it, or this heavy feeling of, “Why did I let that happen again?”\n\nIt’s not like I sit there thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed at myself. I’ll replay it a bit and feel kind of ashamed or frustrated, but the word “guilty” feels… maybe close, but I’m not sure it’s the exact word I’d pick every time.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.\n\nIt’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. Or if it does happen, I’m not recognizing it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of losing control, like you described with eating, happens in any other activities or behaviors besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.

It usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.

And then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. So… yeah, not every time I have one of those eating episodes, but a few times a month, I’d say.

And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not after every single one, no. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.

But for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.

So… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. It’s like the eating happens, then I feel guilty or scared, and then I start thinking I have to undo it somehow.

I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.\n\nIt usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.\n\nBut for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.\n\nSo… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. Yeah… I think if I’m being honest, yes. Not every single time, like I don’t want to make it sound like there’s some exact rule I follow, but for the last few months it’s been happening most weeks.

Usually there’ll be one of those nights where I feel like I ate in this out-of-control way, and then afterward I get this really strong feeling that I have to do something to make up for it. Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and other times I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to go on a long walk even if I’m tired or it’s inconvenient.

So… yes, after most of those episodes lately, there’s usually some kind of “fixing it” behavior afterward. It feels almost automatic now, even though I know it’s not really fixing anything.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is that I’ve been having these nights where, after work especially—or sometimes after a tense dinner—I start eating and it’s like… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, but the decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off.\n\nI can be aware of what I’m doing, like I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and part of me is thinking, “You should stop now,” but I can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment. It feels really strange and upsetting afterward, because it’s not like I’m not aware—it’s more like I’m watching myself keep going and I don’t feel fully in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure I’d call it guilt every time. I mean, afterward I definitely feel bad, like really unsettled and disappointed in myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, even if no one saw it, or this heavy feeling of, “Why did I let that happen again?”\n\nIt’s not like I sit there thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed at myself. I’ll replay it a bit and feel kind of ashamed or frustrated, but the word “guilty” feels… maybe close, but I’m not sure it’s the exact word I’d pick every time.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.\n\nIt’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. Or if it does happen, I’m not recognizing it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of losing control, like you described with eating, happens in any other activities or behaviors besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.\n\nIt usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. So… yeah, not every time I have one of those eating episodes, but a few times a month, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors, like making yourself throw up or taking laxatives, after every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Not after every single one, no. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.\n\nBut for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.\n\nSo… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. It’s like the eating happens, then I feel guilty or scared, and then I start thinking I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in compensatory behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after most of your out-of-control eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if I’m being honest, yes. Not every single time, like I don’t want to make it sound like there’s some exact rule I follow, but for the last few months it’s been happening most weeks.\n\nUsually there’ll be one of those nights where I feel like I ate in this out-of-control way, and then afterward I get this really strong feeling that I have to do something to make up for it. Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and other times I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to go on a long walk even if I’m tired or it’s inconvenient.\n\nSo… yes, after most of those episodes lately, there’s usually some kind of “fixing it” behavior afterward. It feels almost automatic now, even though I know it’s not really fixing anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.\n\nIt usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. So… yeah, not every time I have one of those eating episodes, but a few times a month, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors, like making yourself throw up or taking laxatives, after every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Not after every single one, no. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.\n\nBut for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.\n\nSo… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. It’s like the eating happens, then I feel guilty or scared, and then I start thinking I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in compensatory behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after most of your out-of-control eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if I’m being honest, yes. Not every single time, like I don’t want to make it sound like there’s some exact rule I follow, but for the last few months it’s been happening most weeks.\n\nUsually there’ll be one of those nights where I feel like I ate in this out-of-control way, and then afterward I get this really strong feeling that I have to do something to make up for it. Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and other times I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to go on a long walk even if I’m tired or it’s inconvenient.\n\nSo… yes, after most of those episodes lately, there’s usually some kind of “fixing it” behavior afterward. It feels almost automatic now, even though I know it’s not really fixing anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you engage in these compensatory behaviors after almost every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"For the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, and then I try to make up for it by throwing up, taking laxatives, skipping meals, or forcing a long walk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Almost every… yeah, I think that’s probably fair, at least lately. I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I have a spreadsheet in my head or anything, and there are times where I just go to bed feeling terrible and don’t do anything. But for the last few months, most weeks there’s been at least one night where I eat in that out-of-control way, and then afterward I get stuck in this “I have to make up for it” mindset.

Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and sometimes I don’t do either of those but I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to take a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or don’t really have time. It’s like I’m trying to cancel it out somehow.

So… not literally every single episode, but close enough that it feels like part of the pattern now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brought me in is that I’ve been having these nights where, after work especially—or sometimes after a tense dinner—I start eating and it’s like… I don’t know how to explain it exactly, but the decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off.\n\nI can be aware of what I’m doing, like I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and part of me is thinking, “You should stop now,” but I can’t seem to make myself stop in that moment. It feels really strange and upsetting afterward, because it’s not like I’m not aware—it’s more like I’m watching myself keep going and I don’t feel fully in control.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure I’d call it guilt every time. I mean, afterward I definitely feel bad, like really unsettled and disappointed in myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like embarrassment, even if no one saw it, or this heavy feeling of, “Why did I let that happen again?”\n\nIt’s not like I sit there thinking I hurt someone else, if that makes sense. It’s more directed at myself. I’ll replay it a bit and feel kind of ashamed or frustrated, but the word “guilty” feels… maybe close, but I’m not sure it’s the exact word I’d pick every time.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or emotionally upset?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not every single time in a way I can neatly label, but a lot of the time it’s after work or after some tense conversation at dinner, when I’m already wound up or feeling kind of unsettled.\n\nIt’s like I’ll start eating, and at first it seems normal enough, but then something shifts. The decision-making part of me just sort of shuts off. I can see myself reaching for more food, opening something else, going back again, and there’s this part of me thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but I don’t actually stop. It’s like I’m aware of it happening, but I don’t feel fully in control of it in the moment.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say stress or feeling emotionally worked up is often around it, even if I don’t always realize that until afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control the amount of food you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I planned, because I think everyone does that sometimes. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. Or if it does happen, I’m not recognizing it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of losing control, like you described with eating, happens in any other activities or behaviors besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not every single episode, no… I know I keep coming back to that distinction, but it doesn’t feel completely accurate to say every single time. There are times when I just feel awful afterward and sort of sit with it or go to bed anxious and ashamed.

But for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks where I’ll have one of those out-of-control eating nights, and then afterward I feel like I have to do something to make up for it. Sometimes I make myself throw up, sometimes I take laxatives, and sometimes I don’t do either of those but I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to go on a long walk, even if I’m tired or really don’t want to.

So the honest answer is: not after every single one, but it’s close enough and frequent enough that it feels like part of the pattern now.

I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s more like, once it gets going, I can sort of see it happening and I still can’t stop it.\n\nLike some nights after work, or if dinner has been tense, I’ll start with something small and then I’m reaching for more, and then something else, and then going back again. And there’s this very clear part of me thinking, “Okay, that’s enough, stop now,” but it doesn’t translate into actually stopping. It’s almost like the part of me that normally makes decisions just shuts off for a while.\n\nI’m aware of what I’m doing, which almost makes it worse, because I’m not blacked out or anything. I know I’m eating, I know I’m past the point where I wanted to stop, but in the moment it feels like I can’t really get control of the amount. Only afterward do I sort of come back to myself and think, “Why didn’t I just stop?”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you lose control in situations other than eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, not in the same clear way, if that’s what you mean. With eating, I can point to it afterward and say, “Okay, that felt out of control.” In other situations, it’s harder for me to tell.\n\nI don’t usually think of myself as someone who loses control generally. Like, at work I might get stressed or irritated, but I don’t think I’m doing things I can’t stop myself from doing. I might overthink things or get caught up in my head, but that feels different from the eating episodes.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that outside of eating. Or if it does happen, I’m not recognizing it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the feeling of losing control, like you described with eating, happens in any other activities or behaviors besides eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve really noticed. I keep trying to think of examples, like spending money, drinking, arguing, scrolling on my phone, things like that, and it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nI mean, I can definitely get stuck in my head or procrastinate or keep doing something longer than I meant to, but it still feels like I could stop if I really decided to. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.\n\nIt usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. So… yeah, not every time I have one of those eating episodes, but a few times a month, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors, like making yourself throw up or taking laxatives, after every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Not after every single one, no. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.\n\nBut for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.\n\nSo… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. It feels almost automatic now, even though I know it’s not really fixing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors after almost every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Almost every… yeah, I think that’s probably fair, at least lately. Yeah… I mean, I know I already kind of said this, but yes, almost every time lately. With the eating, it has this different quality, where I’m sort of watching myself go past the point I meant to stop and still not stopping. I don’t really recognize that in other parts of my life.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… no, not clearly. If it’s happening somewhere else, I’m not aware of it in the same way.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: For the last few months, it’s been most weeks. Not every single day or anything like that, but usually there’s at least one night in a week where it happens — where I get into that out-of-control eating, and then afterward I panic and try to undo it somehow.\n\nSometimes that means I make myself throw up. Other times I’ll take laxatives, or I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll force myself to go on a really long walk even if I’m tired. I sort of tell myself I’m “balancing it out,” but I know that sounds… I don’t know. It’s become this pattern.\n\nIt’s hard to give an exact number because some weeks are worse than others, but if I’m being honest, lately it’s been happening most weeks, at least once.\n\nDoctor: How often do you engage in behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: It’s usually tied to those nights when I feel like the eating got away from me. So… for the last few months, most weeks that I have one of those episodes, I do something afterward to try to “fix” it, I guess.\n\nIt’s not always the exact same thing every time. Sometimes I make myself throw up, and sometimes I take laxatives. Other times I don’t do those, but I’ll skip meals the next day or tell myself I have to go on a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or inconvenient. It all feels like the same kind of reaction afterward, like I’m panicking and trying to make up for what happened.\n\nIf I had to put a number on it, I’d say most weeks lately — usually after one of the out-of-control eating nights. Not every day, but it’s been regular enough that I can’t really pretend it’s just a one-off thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself throw up after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think… if I’m only counting actually making myself throw up, not the other stuff, maybe two or three times a month lately? It’s hard to say exactly because I don’t really keep track, and honestly I try not to think about it afterward.\n\nIt usually happens late, after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll go into the bathroom and tell myself I’m just undoing the damage, like if I can get it out then maybe it won’t “count” somehow. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but in the moment it feels really urgent.\n\nAnd then afterward I feel awful — embarrassed, kind of shaky, and like I don’t want anyone to know. So… yeah, not every time I have one of those eating episodes, but a few times a month, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors, like making yourself throw up or taking laxatives, after every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Not after every single one, no. I think that’s important to say because sometimes I’ll have one of those nights where I feel completely out of control with food, and afterward I’m panicking, but I don’t always do the exact same thing.\n\nBut for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that if I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, I’ll do something to try to make up for it. Sometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s laxatives, and sometimes it’s more like I’ll skip meals the next day or make myself go on a really long walk even if I don’t want to.\n\nSo… not literally every episode, but often enough that it feels like part of the whole cycle now. It’s like the eating happens, then I feel guilty or scared, and then I start thinking I have to undo it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in compensatory behaviors like making yourself throw up, taking laxatives, or skipping meals after most of your out-of-control eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if I’m being honest, yes. Not every single time, like I don’t want to make it sound like there’s some exact rule I follow, but for the last few months it’s been happening most weeks.\n\nUsually there’ll be one of those nights where I feel like I ate in this out-of-control way, and then afterward I get this really strong feeling that I have to do something to make up for it. Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and other times I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to go on a long walk even if I’m tired or it’s inconvenient.\n\nSo… yes, after most of those episodes lately, there’s usually some kind of “fixing it” behavior afterward. It feels almost automatic now, even though I know it’s not really fixing anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in these compensatory behaviors after almost every episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Almost every… yeah, I think that’s probably fair, at least lately. It’s like I’m trying to cancel it out somehow.\n\nSo… not literally every single episode, but close enough that it feels like part of the pattern now.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in compensatory behaviors after every single episode of out-of-control eating?\n\nPatient: Not every single episode, no… I know I keep coming back to that distinction, but it doesn’t feel completely accurate to say every single time. I keep wanting to qualify it because it’s not like I have a spreadsheet in my head or anything, and there are times where I just go to bed feeling terrible and don’t do anything. But for the last few months, most weeks there’s been at least one night where I eat in that out-of-control way, and then afterward I get stuck in this “I have to make up for it” mindset.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself throw up, sometimes it’s taking laxatives, and sometimes I don’t do either of those but I’ll skip meals the next day or force myself to take a really long walk, even if I’m exhausted or don’t really have time. Not literally every single episode, because there are times I just feel horrible and don’t do anything right away, but for the last few months it’s been most weeks that I’ll have one of those nights where eating feels out of control, and then afterward I get this panicky need to make up for it somehow.\n\nSometimes that’s making myself throw up. Sometimes it’s taking laxatives. Other times I’ll skip meals the next day, or I’ll make myself go on a really long walk even if I’m exhausted or it’s late or I don’t really have the time. It’s like I’m trying to undo it, even though part of me knows it doesn’t really work that way.\n\nSo… yes, after almost every one of those episodes, there’s usually some kind of compensating thing I do.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you engage in compensatory behaviors after every episode of out-of-control eating, or are there episodes where you don't do anything to compensate?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"For the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks that I have one of those out-of-control eating nights, and then I try to make up for it by throwing up, taking laxatives, skipping meals, or forcing a long walk.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
There are some episodes where I don’t do anything, yes. I don’t want to make it sound like it’s literally every single time, because that wouldn’t be completely true.

But for the last few months, it’s been happening most weeks where I’ll have one of those nights where the eating feels really out of control, and then afterward I get this strong feeling that I have to make up for it somehow. Sometimes that means making myself throw up, sometimes taking laxatives, and sometimes I don’t do those but I’ll skip meals afterward or force myself to go on a long walk, even if I’m tired or don’t really have the time.

So… not every episode, but it’s frequent enough that it feels like part of the same cycle now.
